I'm Dr. [Name], a psychiatrist. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, Doctor. I came in because, honestly, I just can’t shut my brain off anymore. It usually starts with just one small concern—like a client not responding to an email. But then, within minutes, my mind just runs with it. I'll be completely convinced that I’m going to lose the contract, and then I start worrying that I'll fall behind on my divorce payments, and that I'll just never get back to a real leadership role at work. It just spirals out of control, and I can't seem to stop it.\n\nDoctor: When your mind starts spiraling like this with worry, do you notice any physical symptoms—like your heart racing, sweating, trembling, or trouble breathing?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed anything like that. It's just been a constant thing since I lost that job.\n\nDoctor: You've mentioned worries about your career, finances, and what people think of you. Are you also finding yourself worrying excessively about other areas of your life—like your health, your relationships, or how you're managing day-to-day responsibilities?\n\nPatient: No, not really. It just spirals out of control, and I can't seem to stop it.\n\nDoctor: When your mind starts spiraling like this with worry, do you notice any physical symptoms—like your heart racing, sweating, trembling, or trouble breathing?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed anything like that. Um, my heart doesn't race, and I don't get sweaty or have trouble breathing. It's... it's mostly just my thoughts going a mile a minute. The spiraling really just stays in my head, I guess. I haven't had those physical reactions you mentioned.\n\nDoctor: When you have these worries spiraling in your mind, are they mostly about things that might happen in the future, or are they centered around specific situations that you find yourself avoiding or staying away from?\n\nPatient: It's definitely about things that might happen in the future. I don't really find myself avoiding specific places or situations, it's more that... my mind just keeps jumping from one thing to the next. \n\nLike, I'll be worrying about my current contract work, and then it immediately shifts to how I'm going to make my mortgage payments, and then I start obsessing over whether people in the Bethesda tech circles think I'm damaged goods now. It just builds on itself. If a recruiter delays sending an email, it doesn't just mean they're busy—to me, it can feel like absolute proof that my whole career is closing down. So, yeah, it's mostly just future \"what-ifs\" constantly looping in my head.\n\nDoctor: When you're having these worries about the future, do you find it difficult to control them or stop them once they start?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it is extremely difficult. Like I mentioned when we first started talking, I really just can't shut my brain off anymore. Once that first little concern pops up, I just can't stop it. It takes off, and I can't control it at all. \n\nI try to reel it in, but before I know it, I'm already panicking about losing the contract and missing my divorce payments and ruining my career. I just... I can't seem to put the brakes on it once it gets going.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been dealing with this constant worry and racing thoughts that you can't control? Has this been going on for weeks, months, or longer?\n\nPatient: It's been going on for... well, over a year now. About thirteen months, I'd say. It all started when I was fired from my last job. I figured the worrying would stop once I got some income flowing again, but it never really let up, even after I found this contract work I'm doing now. It's just been a constant thing since I lost that job.\n\nDoctor: You've mentioned worries about your career, finances, and what people think of you. Are you also finding yourself worrying excessively about other areas of your life—like your health, your relationships, or how you're managing day-to-day responsibilities?\n\nPatient: No, not really. It's pretty much just focused on work and money. I don't really worry about my health, and I don't have a lot of relationships to worry about right now anyway, with the divorce and everything. Day-to-day stuff is fine, I get my groceries and pay my bills on time. It's just the career and financial stuff that completely takes over my mind.\n\nDoctor: How much is this constant worrying affecting your daily life? For example, is it interfering with your ability to work, sleep, or concentrate on tasks?\n\nPatient: It's affecting things quite a bit, honestly. With work... well, I've actually delayed a few client deliverables recently. I just sit there and get completely stuck anticipating every single thing that could go wrong, so I can't seem to finish the task. I've also missed some important networking follow-ups because my mind is just too tied up in all that. \n\nAnd outside of work, I've pretty much pulled back from my regular stuff. I used to play soccer and hang out with friends, but I've stopped going. I'm just... I'm embarrassed by how tense and irritable I've become, so I just stay away. So yeah, it's definitely getting in the way of things.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned the worrying is mostly about work and money. But let me ask more broadly—when you're not actively thinking about those specific concerns, do you find yourself worrying about other things in general, or does your mind pretty much stay quiet unless something work or finance-related triggers it?\n\nPatient: I don't really think I worry about much else. It really is just the work and the money. I mean... I guess I'm almost always thinking about those things, so my mind rarely gets a chance to just be quiet anyway. But if I do manage to get distracted from the financial stuff, no, I'm not sitting there worrying about other random things. It just always seems to circle back to my career and my bank account.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

I don't really find myself avoiding specific places or situations, it's more that... my mind just keeps jumping from one thing to the next. \n\nLike, I'll be worrying about my current contract work, and then it immediately shifts to how I'm going to make my mortgage payments, and then I start obsessing over whether people in the Bethesda tech circles think I'm damaged goods now. Like I mentioned when we first started talking, I really just can't shut my brain off anymore. Once that first little concern pops up, I just can't stop it. It's just the career and financial stuff that completely takes over my mind.\n\nDoctor: How much is this constant worrying affecting your daily life? Well, yeah, the divorce does get in there too. I mean, my mind just doesn't stop. It keeps jumping around... from the contract work I'm doing, to how I'm going to make the mortgage payments, and then, yeah, to the divorce and what people are thinking. So, yes, the personal stuff definitely gets mixed in with the rest of the worry.

It usually starts with just one small concern—like a client not responding to an email. But then, within minutes, my mind just runs with it. I'll be completely convinced that I’m going to lose the contract, and then I start worrying that I'll fall behind on my divorce payments, and that I'll just never get back to a real leadership role at work. It just spirals out of control, and I can't seem to stop it.\n\nDoctor: When your mind starts spiraling like this with worry, do you notice any physical symptoms—like your heart racing, sweating, trembling, or trouble breathing?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed anything like that. Um, my heart doesn't race, and I don't get sweaty or have trouble breathing. It's... it's mostly just my thoughts going a mile a minute. The spiraling really just stays in my head, I guess. I haven't had those physical reactions you mentioned.\n\nDoctor: When you have these worries spiraling in your mind, are they mostly about things that might happen in the future, or are they centered around specific situations that you find yourself avoiding or staying away from?\n\nPatient: It's definitely about things that might happen in the future. I don't really find myself avoiding specific places or situations, it's more that... my mind just keeps jumping from one thing to the next. \n\nLike, I'll be worrying about my current contract work, and then it immediately shifts to how I'm going to make my mortgage payments, and then I start obsessing over whether people in the Bethesda tech circles think I'm damaged goods now. It just builds on itself. If a recruiter delays sending an email, it doesn't just mean they're busy—to me, it can feel like absolute proof that my whole career is closing down. So, yeah, it's mostly just future \"what-ifs\" constantly looping in my head.\n\nDoctor: When you're having these worries about the future, do you find it difficult to control them or stop them once they start?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it is extremely difficult. Like I mentioned when we first started talking, I really just can't shut my brain off anymore. Once that first little concern pops up, I just can't stop it. It takes off, and I can't control it at all. \n\nI try to reel it in, but before I know it, I'm already panicking about losing the contract and missing my divorce payments and ruining my career. I just... I can't seem to put the brakes on it once it gets going.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been dealing with this constant worry and racing thoughts that you can't control? Has this been going on for weeks, months, or longer?\n\nPatient: It's been going on for... well, over a year now. About thirteen months, I'd say. It all started when I was fired from my last job. I figured the worrying would stop once I got some income flowing again, but it never really let up, even after I found this contract work I'm doing now. It's just been a constant thing since I lost that job.\n\nDoctor: You've mentioned worries about your career, finances, and what people think of you. Are you also finding yourself worrying excessively about other areas of your life—like your health, your relationships, or how you're managing day-to-day responsibilities?\n\nPatient: No, not really. It's pretty much just focused on work and money. I don't really worry about my health, and I don't have a lot of relationships to worry about right now anyway, with the divorce and everything. Day-to-day stuff is fine, I get my groceries and pay my bills on time. It's just the career and financial stuff that completely takes over my mind.\n\nDoctor: How much is this constant worrying affecting your daily life? For example, is it interfering with your ability to work, sleep, or concentrate on tasks?\n\nPatient: It's affecting things quite a bit, honestly. With work... well, I've actually delayed a few client deliverables recently. I just sit there and get completely stuck anticipating every single thing that could go wrong, so I can't seem to finish the task. I've also missed some important networking follow-ups because my mind is just too tied up in all that. \n\nAnd outside of work, I've pretty much pulled back from my regular stuff. I used to play soccer and hang out with friends, but I've stopped going. I'm just... I'm embarrassed by how tense and irritable I've become, so I just stay away. It really is just the work and the money. I mean... I guess I'm almost always thinking about those things, so my mind rarely gets a chance to just be quiet anyway. I don't really sit around worrying about getting in a car crash on the way to work, or getting sick, or anything like that. It's just a constant loop of career and financial stuff.\n\nDoctor: When you wake up in the morning, before you even think about work or money, do you find yourself feeling anxious or worried about anything at all, or does the anxiety really only kick in once you start thinking about your job and finances?\n\nPatient: Honestly, the anxiety is just... there as soon as I wake up. I don't really find myself avoiding specific places or situations, it's more that... my mind just keeps jumping from one thing to the next. \n\nLike, I'll be worrying about my current contract work, and then it immediately shifts to how I'm going to make my mortgage payments, and then I start obsessing over whether people in the Bethesda tech circles think I'm damaged goods now. It just builds on itself. If a recruiter delays sending an email, it doesn't just mean they're busy—to me, it can feel like absolute proof that my whole career is closing down. So, yeah, it's mostly just future \"what-ifs\" constantly looping in my head.\n\nDoctor: When you're having these worries about the future, do you find it difficult to control them or stop them once they start?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it is extremely difficult. Like I mentioned when we first started talking, I really just can't shut my brain off anymore. Once that first little concern pops up, I just can't stop it. It takes off, and I can't control it at all. \n\nI try to reel it in, but before I know it, I'm already panicking about losing the contract and missing my divorce payments and ruining my career. I just... I can't seem to put the brakes on it once it gets going.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been dealing with this constant worry and racing thoughts that you can't control? Has this been going on for weeks, months, or longer?\n\nPatient: It's been going on for... well, over a year now. About thirteen months, I'd say. It all started when I was fired from my last job. I figured the worrying would stop once I got some income flowing again, but it never really let up, even after I found this contract work I'm doing now. It's just been a constant thing since I lost that job.\n\nDoctor: You've mentioned worries about your career, finances, and what people think of you. Are you also finding yourself worrying excessively about other areas of your life—like your health, your relationships, or how you're managing day-to-day responsibilities?\n\nPatient: No, not really. It's pretty much just focused on work and money. I don't really worry about my health, and I don't have a lot of relationships to worry about right now anyway, with the divorce and everything. Day-to-day stuff is fine, I get my groceries and pay my bills on time. It's just the career and financial stuff that completely takes over my mind.\n\nDoctor: How much is this constant worrying affecting your daily life? For example, is it interfering with your ability to work, sleep, or concentrate on tasks?\n\nPatient: It's affecting things quite a bit, honestly. With work... well, I've actually delayed a few client deliverables recently. I just sit there and get completely stuck anticipating every single thing that could go wrong, so I can't seem to finish the task. I've also missed some important networking follow-ups because my mind is just too tied up in all that. \n\nAnd outside of work, I've pretty much pulled back from my regular stuff. I used to play soccer and hang out with friends, but I've stopped going. I'm just... I'm embarrassed by how tense and irritable I've become, so I just stay away. So yeah, it's definitely getting in the way of things.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned the worrying is mostly about work and money. But let me ask more broadly—when you're not actively thinking about those specific concerns, do you find yourself worrying about other things in general, or does your mind pretty much stay quiet unless something work or finance-related triggers it?\n\nPatient: I don't really think I worry about much else. It really is just the work and the money. I mean... I guess I'm almost always thinking about those things, so my mind rarely gets a chance to just be quiet anyway. But if I do manage to get distracted from the financial stuff, no, I'm not sitting there worrying about other random things. It just always seems to circle back to my career and my bank account.\n\nDoctor: When you think about your day ahead—say tomorrow—do you find yourself worrying about multiple different things that could go wrong, or is it really just the work and money concerns that come to mind?\n\nPatient: No, it really is just the work and money. If I think about tomorrow, I'm thinking about the projects I'm behind on, or what my boss is going to say, or how I'm going to afford my expenses if I lose this job. I'm not... I don't really sit around worrying about getting in a car crash on the way to work, or getting sick, or anything like that. It's just a constant loop of career and financial stuff.\n\nDoctor: When you wake up in the morning, before you even think about work or money, do you find yourself feeling anxious or worried about anything at all, or does the anxiety really only kick in once you start thinking about your job and finances?\n\nPatient: Honestly, the anxiety is just... there as soon as I wake up. I don't even have to be thinking about work or money yet. My eyes open, and I just feel this heavy, tense feeling right away. It's like my body is already wound up before my brain even has a chance to focus on the job or the bills. So, yeah, I guess I do feel anxious before the specific thoughts even start.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned feeling anxious as soon as you wake up, even before thinking about work or money. When you have that heavy, tense feeling in the morning, or at other times when you're not focused on your job or finances, what kinds of things—if any—does your mind tend to worry about?\n\nPatient: I'm really not thinking about anything specific in those first few moments. It's just... it's just a physical feeling. My mind is kind of blank right when I wake up, but my body is already tense. \n\nI don't have thoughts about my health, or my family, or anything else. It's just this heavy dread sitting there. And then, usually within a minute or so, my brain just locks right back onto the IT projects I'm managing or my bank account, and the cycle starts all over again. But yeah, to answer your question, there aren't really any other specific topics I'm worrying about. It's just the feeling itself until the work thoughts take over.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that the heavy, tense feeling is there when you wake up, but then your thoughts quickly turn to work and money. If you were to take a week completely off work—say, you had a vacation planned and all your projects were covered—do you think that anxious, tense feeling would go away, or would it still be there even without those work and financial worries on your mind?\n\nPatient: I honestly don't know. I haven't taken a real vacation where I completely unplugged in... well, a really long time. Even when I do take a few days off, I'm usually still checking my email or worrying that things are falling apart at the office while I'm gone. \n\nSo, I'm not really sure if that physical feeling in the morning would go away or not. It's hard to even imagine a scenario where my projects are actually completely covered and I'm not thinking about them at all. I just haven't been in that situation to know what my body would do.\n\nDoctor: Over the past year, aside from work and money concerns, have there been times when you felt that same heavy, tense, anxious feeling even when nothing work-related or financial was happening—like during a quiet evening at home, or while doing something enjoyable?\n\nPatient: I don't think so, no. Like I said before, it really just revolves around the IT projects and the bills. Even on a quiet evening at home, if I'm feeling that tension, it's because my mind is on work or money. I haven't really noticed feeling that way if those things aren't on my mind. But, to be honest, it's rare that those things aren't on my mind.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned your divorce is ongoing. When you think about your divorce or your personal relationships, do you find yourself worrying or feeling anxious about those things, or does your mind not really go there?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, the divorce does get in there too. I mean, my mind just doesn't stop. It keeps jumping around... from the contract work I'm doing, to how I'm going to make the mortgage payments, and then, yeah, to the divorce and what people are thinking. I worry a lot about whether people in the Bethesda tech circles think I'm damaged goods because of it all. It's like everything is connected and everything is a disaster waiting to happen. Just... a delayed email from a recruiter, for example, can suddenly feel like absolute proof that my whole career is closing down and I'm going to lose everything. So, yes, the personal stuff definitely gets mixed in with the rest of the worry.\n\nDoctor: When you think about the divorce itself—like the emotional aspects of the relationship ending, or how you're coping with that personally—do you find yourself worrying about those things, or is your worry about the divorce mainly tied to the financial and career consequences it's causing?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... I guess it's mostly the financial and career stuff. I mean, the emotional part is there, sure, it sucks that it didn't work out. But when I'm lying awake at night or when my stomach is in knots, it's not really about missing her or the relationship ending. \n\nIt's more about... how much is this going to cost me? How is this going to affect my reputation? If I have to split everything, how am I going to keep the house? That's the stuff that really gets my mind spinning out of control. The actual relationship ending... I think I've kind of processed that. It's the fallout that I can't stop worrying about.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.
